sleep vs obesity

Author

Larry Hollingsworth jr

Introduction

Among U.S. adults aged 20 years and older, is sleep duration associated with obesity after adjusting for demographic, socioeconomic, and selected behavioral factors?

Load Datasets

NoteAnalysis note

The files are NHANES component datasets. SEQN is the participant identifier used to combine records across components. The glimpse() calls are useful checks that each file imported and that the variables needed later are present; the different row counts are expected because not every participant completed every NHANES component.

sleep <- read_xpt("SLQ_L_.xpt")
body_measurements <- read_xpt("BMX_L_.xpt")
demo <-  read_xpt("DEMO_L_.xpt")
diab <-  read_xpt("DIQ_L.xpt")
income <-  read_xpt("INQ_L.xpt")
alcohol <-  read_xpt("ALQ_L.xpt")
smoke <- read_xpt("SMQ_L.xpt")
mental <- read_xpt("DPQ_L.xpt")
occupation <- read_xpt("OCQ_L.xpt")

##subgroup/categorize

bmi_group obese/outcome group

NoteAnalysis note

This section creates the BMI-based outcome categories. The binary obese variable used in the logistic regression represents BMI ≥30 versus BMI <30 among adults with a valid measured BMI. This is a classification based on measured BMI, not a clinical diagnosis.

body_measurements1 <- body_measurements1 |>
  mutate(
    bmi_group = case_when(
      bmi < 18.5             ~ "Underweight",
      bmi >= 18.5 & bmi < 25 ~ "Normal weight",
      bmi >= 25 & bmi < 30   ~ "Overweight",
      bmi >= 30              ~ "Obesity",
      TRUE                   ~ NA_character_
    ),
    obese = case_when(
      is.na(bmi) ~ NA_integer_,
      bmi >= 30  ~ 1L,
      TRUE       ~ 0L
    )
  )

height group

body_measurements1  <- body_measurements1  |>
  mutate(
    height_group = case_when(
      height < 155 ~ "Below average",
      height >= 155 & height <= 165 ~ "Average",
      height > 165 ~ "Above average",
      TRUE ~ NA_character_
    )
  )

###smoking group

smoke1 <- smoke1 |>
  mutate(
    smoking_status = case_when(
      smoked_at_least_100_cigs_in_life == 2 ~ "Never",
      smoked_at_least_100_cigs_in_life == 1 & do_you_smoke_cigs %in% c(1, 2) ~ "Current",
      smoked_at_least_100_cigs_in_life == 1 & do_you_smoke_cigs == 3 ~ "Former",
      TRUE ~ NA_character_
    ),
    smoking_status = factor(smoking_status,
                            levels = c("Never", "Former", "Current"))
  )

Alcohol group

NoteAnalysis note

The alcohol categories collapse the original frequency responses into broader groups. This simplifies descriptive and regression analyses, but it also loses detail within each category. The “none in past year” group is the reference category in the full model.

alcohol1 <- alcohol1 |>
  mutate(
    alcohol_group = case_when(
      how_often_drink %in% c(77, 99) ~ NA_character_,
      how_often_drink == 0           ~ "None in past year",
      how_often_drink %in% 7:10      ~ "Infrequent (monthly or less)",
      how_often_drink %in% 5:6       ~ "Occasional (weekly to 2-3 times/month)",
      how_often_drink %in% 1:4       ~ "Frequent (2 or more times/week)",
      TRUE                           ~ NA_character_
    ),
    alcohol_group = factor(alcohol_group,
                           levels = c("None in past year",
                                      "Infrequent (monthly or less)",
                                      "Occasional (weekly to 2-3 times/month)",
                                      "Frequent (2 or more times/week)"))
  )
table(alcohol1$alcohol_group, useNA = "ifany")

                     None in past year           Infrequent (monthly or less) 
                                   836                                   1682 
Occasional (weekly to 2-3 times/month)        Frequent (2 or more times/week) 
                                   994                                   1405 
                                  <NA> 
                                  1420

Income group

NoteAnalysis note

The income groups are derived from the family monthly poverty-income ratio measure used in this code. These are project-defined analytic categories; do not describe them as official program eligibility categories unless that has been independently verified.

income1 <- income1 |>
  mutate(
    income_group = case_when(
      income8 <= 1.30 ~ "Very low income",
      income8 > 1.30 & income8 <= 1.85 ~ "Low income",
      income8 > 1.85 ~ "Above low-income",
      TRUE ~ NA_character_
    )
  )

table(income1$income_group, useNA = "ifany")

Above low-income       Low income  Very low income             <NA> 
            5038             1353             2598             2944

Age groups

NoteAnalysis note

Age is grouped into 20–29, 30–44, 45–59, 60–74, and 75+ years. The 20–29 group is the reference category in the regression models. Grouping age makes results easier to communicate but reduces the detail available from treating age as a continuous variable.

demo1 <- demo1 |>
  mutate(
    age_group = case_when(
      age >= 20 & age < 30 ~ "20–29",
      age >= 30 & age < 45 ~ "30–44",
      age >= 45 & age < 60 ~ "45–59",
      age >= 60 & age < 75 ~ "60–74",
      age >= 75            ~ "75+",
      TRUE                 ~ NA_character_
    )
  )

table(demo1$age_group, useNA = "ifany")

20–29 30–44 45–59 60–74   75+  <NA> 
  904  1760  1641  2513   991  4124

Exposure group

NoteAnalysis note

Sleep duration is based on the NHANES weekday/workday usual sleep-hours variable. The categories are short sleep (<7 hours), recommended sleep (7–9 hours), and long sleep (>9 hours). Recommended sleep is set as the reference group, so the model odds ratios compare short and long sleep with the 7–9-hour group. These are self-reported hours, not objective sleep measurements.

sleep1 <- sleep1 |>
  mutate(
    sleep_hours = as.numeric(sleep_hours),

    sleep_group = case_when(
      sleep_hours < 7                     ~ "Short sleep (<7 hours)",
      sleep_hours >= 7 & sleep_hours <= 9 ~ "Recommended sleep (7–9 hours)",
      sleep_hours > 9                     ~ "Long sleep (>9 hours)",
      TRUE                                ~ NA_character_
    ),

    sleep_group = factor(sleep_group,
                         levels = c("Recommended sleep (7–9 hours)",
                                    "Short sleep (<7 hours)",
                                    "Long sleep (>9 hours)"))
  )

table(sleep1$sleep_group, useNA = "ifany")

Recommended sleep (7–9 hours)        Short sleep (<7 hours) 
                         5612                          1736 
        Long sleep (>9 hours)                          <NA> 
                         1040                           113 
class(sleep1$sleep_hours)
[1] "numeric"
class(sleep1$sleep_group)
[1] "factor"
levels(sleep1$sleep_group)
[1] "Recommended sleep (7–9 hours)" "Short sleep (<7 hours)"       
[3] "Long sleep (>9 hours)"

###smoking status variable

smoke1 <- smoke1 |>
  mutate(
    smoking_status = case_when(
      smoked_at_least_100_cigs_in_life == 2 ~ "Never",
      smoked_at_least_100_cigs_in_life == 1 & do_you_smoke_cigs %in% c(1, 2) ~ "Current",
      smoked_at_least_100_cigs_in_life == 1 & do_you_smoke_cigs == 3 ~ "Former",
      TRUE ~ NA_character_
    ),
    smoking_status = factor(smoking_status,
                            levels = c("Never", "Former", "Current"))
  )

##Merge and filter ###merge into df

df <- sleep1 |>
  left_join(body_measurements1, by = "SEQN") |>
  left_join(demo1, by = "SEQN") |>
  left_join(diab1, by = "SEQN") |>
  left_join(income1, by = "SEQN") |>
  left_join(alcohol1, by = "SEQN") |> 
  left_join(smoke1, by = "SEQN") |> 
  left_join(occupation1, by="SEQN") |> 
  left_join(mental1, by="SEQN")

merge + filter 20 ages

NoteAnalysis note

The component files are joined by SEQN, and the analytic population is restricted to adults aged 20 years or older. Keep this age definition consistent in the research question, plots, labels, and written interpretation; do not refer to the sample as adults 18+.

df1 <- df |>
  select(
    height,
    arm_circumference,
    waist_circumference,
    income_group,

###Value labels

df1 <- df1 |>
  mutate(
    race = factor(race, levels = 1:5,
                  labels = c("Mexican American", "Other Hispanic", "Non-Hispanic White",
                             "Non-Hispanic Black", "Other race, including multiracial")),
    gender = factor(gender, levels = 1:2, labels = c("Male", "Female")),
    education = factor(education, levels = 1:5,
                       labels = c("Less than 9th grade", "9th-11th grade",
                                  "High school graduate or GED",
                                  "Some college or associate degree",
                                  "College graduate or above")),
    marital_stat = factor(marital_stat, levels = 1:3,
                          labels = c("Married or living with partner",
                                     "Widowed, divorced, or separated", "Never married")),
    diabetes = factor(diabetes, levels = 1:3, labels = c("Yes", "No", "Borderline")),
    prediabetes = factor(prediabetes, levels = 1:2, labels = c("Yes", "No")),
    feeling_depressed = factor(feeling_depressed, levels = 0:3,
                               labels = c("Not at all", "Several days",
                                          "More than half the days", "Nearly every day")),
    usually_works_35plus_hours = factor(usually_works_35plus_hours, levels = 1:2,
                                        labels = c("Yes", "No"))
  )

##univariate analysis

NoteSummary

The analytic file includes 7,809 adults aged 20 years and older. All counts and percentages in this section are unweighted and describe the sample, not the U.S. population. Percentages use all 7,809 adults as the denominator, so missing values appear as their own row.

##numeric variable function

numerical_summary <- function(data, variable) {
  data |>
    summarise(
      mean_x = mean({{ variable }}, na.rm = TRUE),
      median_x = median({{ variable }}, na.rm = TRUE),
      sd_x = sd({{ variable }}, na.rm = TRUE),
      min_x = min({{ variable }}, na.rm = TRUE),
      max_x = max({{ variable }}, na.rm = TRUE)
    )
}

age numeric summary

NoteAnalysis note

The numeric summaries describe the distribution before categorization. Report the age range and summary statistics only if they are relevant to the study population description.

NoteSummary

Age was available for all 7,809 participants. The mean age was 53.6 years (SD 17.5) and the median was 57.0 years, ranging from 20 to 80. NHANES top-codes age at 80, so the maximum includes everyone aged 80 or older. The median above the mean shows the sample leans toward older adults.

numerical_summary(df1,age)
# A tibble: 1 × 5
  mean_x median_x  sd_x min_x max_x
   <dbl>    <dbl> <dbl> <dbl> <dbl>
1   53.6       57  17.5    20    80

height numeric summary

NoteSummary

Measured height was available for 5,997 adults (1,812 missing, 23.2%). The mean height was 166.9 cm (SD 10.1), median 166.5 cm, range 133.0–200.7 cm. Missing height mostly reflects participants who completed the interview but not the physical examination.

numerical_summary(df1,height)
# A tibble: 1 × 5
  mean_x median_x  sd_x min_x max_x
   <dbl>    <dbl> <dbl> <dbl> <dbl>
1   167.     166.  10.1   133  201.

###bmi numeric summary

NoteSummary

Measured BMI was available for 5,970 adults (1,839 missing, 23.5%). The mean BMI was 29.8 kg/m² (SD 7.4), just under the obesity cutoff of 30, and the median was 28.5 kg/m², ranging from 11.1 to 74.8. The mean above the median shows a right skew from a tail of very high BMI values.

numerical_summary(df1,bmi)
# A tibble: 1 × 5
  mean_x median_x  sd_x min_x max_x
   <dbl>    <dbl> <dbl> <dbl> <dbl>
1   29.8     28.5  7.36  11.1  74.8

##categorical summary function

categorical_summary <- function(data, variable) {
  data |>
    count({{ variable }}) |>
    mutate(percent = round(n / sum(n) * 100, 1))
}

sleep group/ exposure variable categorical summary

NoteAnalysis note

The sleep-group counts show how participants are distributed across the exposure categories. These are unweighted counts unless the code explicitly applies survey weights; do not interpret them as nationally representative percentages.

NoteSummary

Most adults reported recommended weekday sleep of 7–9 hours (5,154; 66.0%). Short sleep under 7 hours was reported by 1,636 (21.0%) and long sleep over 9 hours by 908 (11.6%). Sleep duration was missing for 111 (1.4%). The exposure has few missing values, and both comparison groups are large enough for analysis.

categorical_summary(df1, sleep_group)
# A tibble: 4 × 3
  sleep_group                       n percent
  <fct>                         <int>   <dbl>
1 Recommended sleep (7–9 hours)  5154    66  
2 Short sleep (<7 hours)         1636    21  
3 Long sleep (>9 hours)           908    11.6
4 <NA>                            111     1.4

obese group/ outcome variable categorical summary

NoteAnalysis note

The outcome summary describes the number classified as obese and non-obese among participants with valid BMI.

NoteSummary

Of the 7,809 adults, 2,455 (31.4%) were classified as obese (BMI 30 or higher) and 3,515 (45.0%) as not obese. Obesity status was missing for 1,839 (23.5%) because BMI was not measured. Among the 5,970 adults with a measured BMI, 41.1% were obese (unweighted).

###alcohol group

NoteSummary

Among adults with alcohol data, the largest group drank infrequently (monthly or less; 1,589; 20.3%), followed by frequent drinking of 2 or more times per week (1,396; 17.9%), occasional drinking (965; 12.4%), and no drinking in the past year (820; 10.5%). Alcohol use was missing for 3,039 adults (38.9%), the highest missingness of any model variable. This includes about 460 lifetime non-drinkers who skipped the frequency question, as well as participants who did not complete the alcohol questionnaire.

Bivariate analysis

NoteAnalysis note

The bivariate analysis screens each candidate variable for an association with obesity before the multivariable models. These tests are descriptive/exploratory and do not establish that a variable is a confounder or that an association is causal.

Setup

chi_vars <- c("sleep_group", "age_group", "income_group", "alcohol_group",
              "height_group", "diabetes", "prediabetes", "race", "education",
              "marital_stat", "gender", "usually_works_35plus_hours",
              "feeling_depressed", "smoking_status")

Analytic dataset

NoteAnalysis note

The bivariate dataset includes participants with a positive MEC examination weight. Special NHANES response codes are recoded to missing where applicable (for example, 7/9 or 77/99). This prevents “refused,” “don’t know,” or other non-substantive codes from being treated as real categories.

df1_w <- df1 |>
  filter(!is.na(WTMEC2YR), WTMEC2YR > 0) |>
  mutate(
    diabetes     = replace(diabetes,     diabetes %in% 9, NA),
    education    = replace(education,    education %in% 9, NA),
    marital_stat = replace(marital_stat, marital_stat %in% c(77, 99), NA),
    prediabetes  = replace(prediabetes,  prediabetes %in% 9, NA),
    feeling_depressed          = replace(feeling_depressed, feeling_depressed %in% c(7, 9), NA),
    usually_works_35plus_hours = replace(usually_works_35plus_hours,
                                         usually_works_35plus_hours %in% c(7, 9), NA)
  )

Unweighted screening for small cells

NoteAnalysis note

The unweighted chi-square checks are used here to identify sparse cells before the survey-weighted tests. In this output, no variable met the stated small-cell flag (minimum observed count <10 or minimum expected count <5). The unweighted checks are diagnostics, not the final inferential tests.

run_chi <- function(var) {
  d    <- df1_w |> filter(!is.na(.data[[var]]), !is.na(obese))
  tab  <- table(d[[var]], d$obese)
  test <- suppressWarnings(chisq.test(tab))

  data.frame(
    variable     = var,
    n            = sum(tab),
    min_observed = min(tab),
    min_expected = round(min(test$expected), 1)
  )
}

chi_results <- bind_rows(lapply(chi_vars, run_chi))

Survey design for weighted analysis

NoteAnalysis note

The survey design incorporates NHANES strata (SDMVSTRA), primary sampling units (SDMVPSU), and MEC examination weights (WTMEC2YR). Accounting for these features is necessary for appropriate variance estimation and population-level inference from the examination sample.

des_all <- svydesign(ids = ~SDMVPSU, strata = ~SDMVSTRA,
                     weights = ~WTMEC2YR, nest = TRUE, data = df1_w)

Weighted tests

NoteAnalysis note

svychisq() provides design-adjusted tests of association between each candidate variable and obesity. These p-values account for the complex survey design. A small p-value indicates evidence of an association in the bivariate analysis, not an adjusted or causal relationship.

run_svychi <- function(var) {
  d    <- subset(des_all, !is.na(des_all$variables[[var]]) & !is.na(obese))
  test <- svychisq(as.formula(paste("~", var, "+ obese")), d)

  data.frame(
    variable = var,
    F_stat   = round(unname(test$statistic), 2),
    p_value  = test$p.value
  )
}

svychi_results <- bind_rows(lapply(chi_vars, run_svychi))
rownames(svychi_results) <- NULL    # added: removes the X-squared row names

Combined table

NoteAnalysis note

This table deliberately combines unweighted sample counts (n) with survey-weighted test statistics and p-values. Label that distinction clearly in any presentation or report; the n column is not a weighted population estimate.

Unweighted n with weighted p values.

  mutate(p_value = signif(p_value, 3))

Multivariable analysis

Setup

vars_models <- c("obese", "sleep_group", "age_group", "gender", "race", "education",
                 "income_group", "marital_stat", "alcohol_group", "smoking_status",
                 "feeling_depressed")

Prepare variables and flag complete cases

NoteAnalysis note

Categorical predictors are converted to factors so the models estimate category-specific contrasts. The complete-case flag requires a nonmissing outcome and every predictor listed in vars_models. Consequently, the adjusted model uses only participants with complete data on all included variables.

df_full <- df1_w |>
  mutate(
    # numeric codes that are really categories
    gender            = factor(gender),
    race              = factor(race),
    education         = factor(education),
    marital_stat      = factor(marital_stat),
    feeling_depressed = factor(feeling_depressed),
    smoking_status = factor(smoking_status),

    # character variables, with the reference group listed first
    age_group = factor(age_group,
                       levels = c("20–29", "30–44", "45–59", "60–74", "75+")),
    income_group = factor(income_group,
                          levels = c("Above low-income", "Low income", "Very low income")),
    alcohol_group = factor(alcohol_group,
                           levels = c("None in past year",
                                      "Infrequent (monthly or less)",
                                      "Occasional (weekly to 2-3 times/month)",
                                      "Frequent (2 or more times/week)"))
  ) |>
  mutate(cc = if_all(all_of(vars_models), ~ !is.na(.x)))

sum(df_full$cc)      # analytic sample size
[1] 3780
nrow(df_full)        # all adults with an exam weight
[1] 6064

Survey design

NoteAnalysis note

The survey design is first created using the full eligible dataset and then subset to complete cases. The output reports 3,780 complete cases out of 6,064 adults with a positive MEC weight. The design degrees of freedom are 15. The weighted mean of the binary obese variable is the weighted obesity prevalence in this complete-case analytic sample.

The design is built on the full data, then subset to complete cases so that all PSUs stay in the design.

des_full <- svydesign(
  ids     = ~SDMVPSU,
  strata  = ~SDMVSTRA,
  weights = ~WTMEC2YR,
  nest    = TRUE,
  data    = df_full
)

des <- subset(des_full, cc)

svymean(~obese, des)
         mean     SE
obese 0.41178 0.0177
df_design <- degf(des)
df_design
[1] 15

Unadjusted model

NoteAnalysis note

m_crude is the unadjusted survey-weighted logistic regression. Its odds ratios compare each sleep category with recommended sleep (7–9 hours), without controlling for other predictors. The crude estimates are short sleep OR 1.42 (95% CI 1.06–1.90) and long sleep OR 1.11 (95% CI 0.77–1.61). The overall Wald test for sleep has p=0.0549.

m_crude <- svyglm(obese ~ sleep_group, design = des, family = quasibinomial())

summary(m_crude, df.resid = df_design)

Adjusted model

NoteAnalysis note

m_adj is the FULL adjusted model and it does include socioeconomic variables: education, income group, and marital status. It also adjusts for age group, gender, race, alcohol group, smoking status, and feeling-depressed category. Relative to recommended sleep, short sleep has OR 1.23 (95% CI 0.93–1.63) and long sleep has OR 0.95 (95% CI 0.67–1.32). The overall design-based Wald test for sleep is F(2,15)=1.29, p=0.3038. Neither sleep contrast is statistically significant, and the overall test does not provide evidence of an association after adjustment. This is not proof that no association exists.

m_adj <- svyglm(
  obese ~ sleep_group + age_group + gender + race + education +
    income_group + marital_stat + alcohol_group + smoking_status +
    feeling_depressed,
  design = des,
  family = quasibinomial()
)

summary(m_adj, df.resid = df_design)

Checks

NoteAnalysis note

The full model has 28 estimated coefficients while the survey design has 15 degrees of freedom. The rendered summary also warns that residual degrees of freedom are zero or negative and that some p-values are not defined. Treat the full-model coefficient-level inference cautiously; the overall design-based Wald test and design-based confidence intervals are still shown, but the model is highly parameterized relative to the available design degrees of freedom. This is a precision/stability concern, not a reason to say the model fails to adjust for socioeconomic variables. Both crude and full adjusted models use the same 3,780 complete cases.

Design degrees of freedom and number of model coefficients:

df_design
[1] 15
length(coef(m_adj))
[1] 28

Both models should use the same number of people as sum(df_full$cc):

nobs(m_crude)
[1] 3780
nobs(m_adj)
[1] 3780

Crude versus adjusted

NoteAnalysis note

This comparison isolates how the sleep odds ratios change after adjustment. Short sleep changes from OR 1.42 to OR 1.23, while long sleep changes from OR 1.11 to OR 0.95. The confidence intervals for both adjusted sleep contrasts include 1, and the full-model overall Wald test is not statistically significant. Describe this as attenuation/change in the estimates after adjustment, not as proof that confounding has been eliminated.

Confidence intervals use the design degrees of freedom for both models.

get_or <- function(m, label, ddf) {
  est <- coef(m); ci <- confint(m, ddf = ddf)
  idx <- grep("sleep_group", names(est))
  data.frame(model = label, term = names(est)[idx],
             OR = round(exp(est[idx]), 2),
             lower = round(exp(ci[idx, 1]), 2),
             upper = round(exp(ci[idx, 2]), 2))
}

res <- rbind(get_or(m_crude, "Unadjusted", df_design),
             get_or(m_adj,   "Adjusted",   df_design))
rownames(res) <- NULL
res
       model                              term   OR lower upper
1 Unadjusted sleep_groupShort sleep (<7 hours) 1.42  1.06  1.90
2 Unadjusted  sleep_groupLong sleep (>9 hours) 1.11  0.77  1.61
3   Adjusted sleep_groupShort sleep (<7 hours) 1.23  0.93  1.63
4   Adjusted  sleep_groupLong sleep (>9 hours) 0.95  0.67  1.32

crude vs slim model

NoteAnalysis note

This comparison is between the crude model and the reduced age/gender/race model, not the full m_adj model.

get_or <- function(m, label, ddf) {
  est <- coef(m)
  ci <- confint(m, ddf = ddf)
  
  idx <- grep("sleep_group", names(est))
  
  data.frame(
    model = label,
    term = names(est)[idx],
    OR = round(exp(est[idx]), 2),
    lower = round(exp(ci[idx, 1]), 2),
    upper = round(exp(ci[idx, 2]), 2)
  )
}

res <- rbind(
  get_or(m_crude, "Unadjusted", df_design),
  get_or(n_adj, "Reduced adjustment (age, gender, race)", df_design)
)

rownames(res) <- NULL
res

1                             Unadjusted sleep_groupShort sleep (<7 hours) 1.42
2                             Unadjusted  sleep_groupLong sleep (>9 hours) 1.11
3 Reduced adjustment (age, gender, race) sleep_groupShort sleep (<7 hours) 1.32

  lower upper

##Results

Before adjustment, short sleep was associated with higher odds of obesity compared with 7-9 hours of sleep (OR 1.42, 95% CI 1.06-1.90), while long sleep showed no clear association (OR 1.11, 95% CI 0.77-1.61). After adjusting for age, gender, race, education, income, marital status, alcohol use, smoking, and depressed mood, the short-sleep estimate was attenuated (OR 1.23, 95% CI 0.93-1.63) and the interval included 1. The long-sleep estimate was also near 1 (OR 0.95, 95% CI 0.67-1.32). The overall test for sleep duration was not statistically significant in the adjusted model (p = 0.30). These results do not show a clear association between sleep duration and obesity after adjustment, though the wide intervals mean a modest association cannot be ruled out.

##Limitations

This study has several limitations. First, the data are cross-sectional, so the temporal order of sleep and obesity cannot be established, and the results describe associations rather than causal effects. Reverse causation is plausible, because obesity can itself disrupt sleep. Second, sleep duration was self-reported and based only on usual weekday or workday sleep, so it is subject to recall and reporting error and may not reflect weekend sleep or sleep quality. Obesity was defined by measured BMI of 30 or higher, which is a practical screening measure but does not distinguish fat from lean mass. Third, the adjusted analysis used complete cases only (3,780 of 6,064 adults with an examination weight), mainly because of missing income and alcohol data, and the participants excluded may differ from those included. NHANES also had a low response rate in this cycle, which may affect representativeness despite the survey weights. Fourth, residual confounding is likely. The models did not include physical activity, diet, sleep apnea, shift work, or medication use. Alcohol use captured drinking frequency rather than quantity, and depressed mood was measured with a single questionnaire item rather than a validated score. Fifth, the survey design had only 15 degrees of freedom while the full model estimated 28 coefficients, so confidence intervals and coefficient-level tests for individual covariates should be interpreted cautiously, and precision for the sleep estimates was limited. The adjusted sleep estimates were not statistically significant, which may reflect limited power as well as a true absence of association. Finally, income groups and other categories were defined for this analysis and grouping continuous variables such as age loses information.

  gender = c("1" = "Male", "2" = "Female"),

                "3" = "High school graduate or GED",
                "4" = "Some college or associate degree",
                "5" = "College graduate or above"),

                   "2" = "Widowed, divorced, or separated",
                   "3" = "Never married"),

# Display order for text variables that would otherwise sort alphabetically
level_order <- list(
  bmi_group = c("Underweight", "Normal weight", "Overweight", "Obesity"),
  height_group = c("Below average", "Average", "Above average")
)

var_labels <- c(
  obese = "Obesity (BMI 30+)", sleep_group = "Sleep duration",
  age_group = "Age group", income_group = "Income group",
  alcohol_group = "Alcohol use (frequency)", height_group = "Height group",
  diabetes = "Diabetes", prediabetes = "Prediabetes", race = "Race/ethnicity",
  education = "Education", marital_stat = "Marital status", gender = "Gender",
  usually_works_35plus_hours = "Usually works 35+ hours/week",
  feeling_depressed = "Feeling down or depressed",
  smoking_status = "Smoking status", bmi_group = "BMI category",
  age = "Age (years)", height = "Height (cm)", bmi = "BMI (kg/m2)"
)

get_levels <- function(v) {
  x <- df1_w[[v]]
  if (!is.null(level_order[[v]])) {
    as.character(level_order[[v]])
  } else if (is.factor(x)) {
    levels(x)
  } else {
    as.character(sort(unique(x[!is.na(x)])))
  }
}

label_levels <- function(v, lev) {
  lab <- value_labels[[v]]
  out <- if (is.null(lab)) lev else unname(lab[lev])
  ifelse(is.na(out), lev, out)
}

# 1. Sample flow
sample_flow <- tibble(
  step = c("Adults 20+ in merged file", "With a positive exam weight",
           "Complete data on all model variables"),
  n = c(nrow(df1), nrow(df1_w), sum(df_full$cc))
)

# 2. Overall obesity prevalence (weighted)
prev_all <- svymean(~obese, des_all, na.rm = TRUE)
prev_model <- svymean(~obese, des, na.rm = TRUE)
prev_overall <- tibble(
  sample = c("Adults with exam weight", "Model sample (complete cases)"),
  prevalence_pct = round(100 * c(coef(prev_all), coef(prev_model)), 1),
  se_pct = round(100 * c(SE(prev_all), SE(prev_model)), 2)
)

# 3. Categorical variable summaries: weighted % and unweighted n, missing as its own row
cat_vars <- c("obese", chi_vars, "bmi_group")

cat_summary <- bind_rows(lapply(cat_vars, function(v) {
  lev <- get_levels(v)
  f <- factor(as.character(df1_w[[v]]), levels = lev)
  ok <- !is.na(f)
  wsum <- vapply(seq_along(lev), function(k) sum(w_all[which(f == lev[k])]), numeric(1))
  tibble(
    variable = v,
    level = c(label_levels(v, lev), "Missing"),
    n = c(as.vector(table(f)), sum(!ok)),
    pct_weighted = c(round(100 * wsum / sum(w_all[ok]), 1), NA_real_)
  )
}))

# 4. Numeric variable summaries
num_vars <- c("age", "height", "bmi")

num_summary <- bind_rows(lapply(num_vars, function(v) {
  m <- svymean(as.formula(paste0("~", v)), des_all, na.rm = TRUE)
  x <- df1_w[[v]]
  tibble(
    variable = v,
    n = sum(!is.na(x)),
    n_missing = sum(is.na(x)),
    mean_weighted = round(as.numeric(coef(m)), 1),
    se = round(as.numeric(SE(m)), 2),
    min = min(x, na.rm = TRUE),
    max = max(x, na.rm = TRUE)
  )
}))

# 5. Missingness among adults with an exam weight
id_cols <- c("SEQN", "WTMEC2YR", "SDMVPSU", "SDMVSTRA")
vars_miss <- setdiff(names(df1_w), id_cols)

missingness <- tibble(
  variable = vars_miss,
  n_missing = unname(vapply(vars_miss, function(v) sum(is.na(df1_w[[v]])), numeric(1)))
) |>
  mutate(pct_missing = round(100 * n_missing / nrow(df1_w), 1)) |>
  arrange(desc(pct_missing))

# 6. Bivariate: weighted % obese in each category
obese_vec <- df1_w$obese

bivariate_prev <- bind_rows(lapply(chi_vars, function(v) {
  lev <- get_levels(v)
  f <- factor(as.character(df1_w[[v]]), levels = lev)
  ok <- !is.na(f) & !is.na(obese_vec)
  tibble(
    variable = v,
    level = label_levels(v, lev),
    n = vapply(seq_along(lev), function(k) sum(ok & f == lev[k], na.rm = TRUE), numeric(1)),
    pct_obese_weighted = vapply(seq_along(lev), function(k) {
      i <- which(ok & f == lev[k])
      round(100 * sum(w_all[i] * obese_vec[i]) / sum(w_all[i]), 1)
    }, numeric(1))
  )
}))

bivariate_tests <- final_tbl

# 7. Model odds ratios, using the design degrees of freedom for p values and intervals
tidy_or <- function(m, label, ddf) {
  est <- coef(m)
  se <- sqrt(diag(vcov(m)))
  tibble(
    model = label,
    term = names(est),
    OR = exp(est),
    lower = exp(est - qt(0.975, ddf) * se),
    upper = exp(est + qt(0.975, ddf) * se),
    p_value = 2 * pt(-abs(est / se), ddf)
  )
}

model_or <- bind_rows(
  tidy_or(m_crude, "Unadjusted", df_design),
  tidy_or(n_adj, "Reduced adjustment (age, gender, race)", df_design),
  tidy_or(m_adj, "Fully adjusted", df_design)
) |>
  mutate(across(c(OR, lower, upper), ~ round(.x, 3)),
         p_value = signif(p_value, 3))

# 8. Model details
get_p <- function(rt) if (!is.null(rt$p)) as.numeric(rt$p) else NA_real_
sleep_p <- function(m) get_p(regTermTest(m, "sleep_group", df = df_design))

model_info <- tibble(
  model = c("Unadjusted", "Reduced adjustment (age, gender, race)", "Fully adjusted"),
  n = c(nobs(m_crude), nobs(n_adj), nobs(m_adj)),
  n_coefficients = c(length(coef(m_crude)), length(coef(n_adj)), length(coef(m_adj))),
  design_df = df_design,
  sleep_overall_p = signif(c(sleep_p(m_crude), sleep_p(n_adj), sleep_p(m_adj)), 3)
)

# Save everything the dashboard needs in one file
saveRDS(
  list(
    sample_flow = sample_flow,
    prev_overall = prev_overall,
    cat_summary = cat_summary,
    num_summary = num_summary,

    bivariate_prev = bivariate_prev,
    bivariate_tests = bivariate_tests,
    model_or = model_or,
    model_info = model_info,
    var_labels = var_labels,
    value_labels = value_labels
  ),
  "dashboard/results.rds"
)
